En este concierto admirable, todos los pueblos
se enriquecen en función de una economía mun-
dial normalizada. No se concibe, claro está, una es-
tagnación indefinida en la primera etapa.
Pero, en el ánimo de librarse de los perturba-
dores efectos de la Crisis Mundial, los grandes pue-
blos industriales o capitalistas adoptan una políti-
ca eeonómica muy perjudicial. La ley Hawley-
Smoot 193o y la Conferencia Imperial de Ottawa
1932 . quiebran el ritmo regular del comereio.
sustrayendo los dos mereados más importantes del
mundo: Estados I nidos y el Imperio Británico.
Los pueblos de economía rural se han visto
impelidos a defenderse. Las trabas al comercio in-
ternacional se multiplican. asfixiándolo abrumado-
ramente.

las repúblicas latino-americanas se inician en la

Analicemos los elementos esenciales.
I. — Organización de Expansión Comercial
La atención esmerada y previsora que el Im-
perio concede a su expansión económica, es ex-
iraordinat ia. Formidables entidades de orientación
comercial: generosas primas de exportación; y a-
poyo decidido a las empresas navieras: constitu-
yen el núcleo central de esta política.
Para dar una idea, muy remota desde luego,
de la magnitud de esta protección. confróntese el
contenido esencial de Ja “Ley para el mejoramien-
to de la Marina Mercante".
Dispone la construcción de 200.o00 toneladas
de bareos nuevos para reemplazar naves que ten-
gan más de 25 años de servicios. La forma en que
el Estade Japonés contribuye a esta obra es la si-
gufente: contra la destrucción de dos toneladas de
bareos inservibles, el Gobierno se compromete a o-
torgar un subsídio de 55 yens por cada tonelada
de nueva construcción hecha.
Conviene citar, asimismo. la ley de agosto de
1931. tendiente a la formación de "cartels", a tin
de introducir la fiscalización del Estado en la in-
dustria. Veinticuatro de ellas — la del algodón en-
tre otras — se han agrupado ya.

CAPITULO QUINTO
LAS BARRERAS AL COMERCIO
A. — SU GENESIS
Todos los conceptos e informaciones expues-
los hasta aqui responden únicamente a mi propó-
sito de desarrollar un panorama económico inter-
nacional. que explique la heroica defensa que, de
sus mereados internos. hacen actualmente Jos di-
verses paises.
Por cllo he considerado, sólo muy tangencial-
miente, y en la medida que puedan acarrear alguna
noticia sobre el particular. únicamente los móvi-
les que concurran a la! objetivo.
Examinemos, al efecto, el problema de la des-
ocupación, verdadero flagelo de la estructura eco-
nomica actual.
Se presentan dos cuestiones: hay que impedir
su agravación protegiendo los centros de trabajo

=== Page 7 === (confidence 0.96)
—28-
establecidos) : hay que tender a su extinción pro-
gresiva creando nuevos centros de trabajo.
Ambos remedios suponen un impulso de Ja in-
dustrialización doméstica. que, fatalmente, reper-
cute en una contracción de las importaciones.
La redistribución geográfica de la industria:
l afán de los paises capitalistas por reducir los
saldos desfavorables de sus balanzas comerciales:
la desorbitada expansión comereial japonesa: con-
curren a la necesidad de defender las balanzas de
pagos y orientar el comercio internacional.
Así pacen las barreras al comercio.
B. — SUS CLASES
Las barreras al comercio pueden clasificarso
en cinco grupos:
a Probibiciones absolutas:
b Elevación de derechos aduaneros:
e Protección contra monedas depreciadas:
d. Control de Cambios;
e, Contingentes o Cuotas de Imporfación.
1. — Prohibiciones absolutas
Por medio de ellas, no se permite el ingreso
de tal o cual producto al territorio nacional.
Son sus calusas:
a Razones de indole política o retaliatoria.
b Razones de orden sanitario (enfermedades
de aniniules o plantas. o productos que los eon-
ducen. Es nocivo usarlas como aspecto externo
de una protección aduanera.
e Razones eronómicas (escasez de divisas

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.96)
—29-
 xtranjeras, que determina una prohibición tran-
sitoria de mercaderías no esenciales o fácilmente
sustituibles).
Las prohibiciones absolutas se pueden consi-
derar como recursos supremos, cuya peligrosidad
consiste en las odiosas represalias que engen-
dran.
Il. — Elevación de dereches aduaneres
Obedece a tres motivos determinantes:
it: Obtención de ingresos gubernamentales;
b. Protección a la industria nacional;
 Propósito de nivelar, indirectamente, ia
balanza de pagos.
No me detendré a analizar los varios sistemas
de larifas  la larifa autónoma única; la doble
tarifa autónoma; y la doble tarifa, general y con-
vencional —. porque ello daría tema para una ex-
posición muy dilatada.
Me Jimitaré a consignar que el abuso de la e-
levación de tarifas, es nocivo; pues encarece el
costo de vida. Debe detenerse en el nivel de un ca-
bal proteccionisme.
III. — Proteoción contra monedas depreoiades
Se ha creído eneontrar en esía medida un e-
licaz correctivo contra la depreciación deliberada
de las mouedas. Sabido es que este expediente se ha
tomado como vehículo para competir en los mer-
cados internacionales. Pero, la aplicación de este

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.94)
30一
sistema resultaría injusta, cuando se trata de mo-
nedas depreciadas por causas naturales.
Además, por su propia indole, sólo pueden ser-
virse de clia los países que conservan el patrón de
oro. Y bien poros son estos países; sobre todo hoy.
en que el propio "bloque de oro" vacila y se
desintegra.
IV. — Control de Cambios
Es la reglamentación de las operaciones en
moneda extranjera. Generabmente se emplea paa
coetrelar las importaciones.
Su ejercicio confronta probéemas de gran vo-
humen. a los que no son agenos el entorpecimiet
to de las Iransacciones comerciales ni la estagna-
ción de los créditos extranjeros congelados.
Bolsas negras à una especulactón mmonstroo
st son sts complemeutos obligades.
Comienza hoy a atenuat se el sistema, hacién
dolo funcionar al lado de mercados Jibres de mo-
neda extranjera, que no provenga de teles o cuales
exportaciones.
V. — Los Contingentes o Cuotas
Por ser el objetivo princípal de este trabajo.
to trataré en capitulo aparte.

SEGUNDA PARTE
EL SISTEMA DE CONTINGENTES O CUOTAS
CAPITULO PRIMERO
SU NATURALEZA Y CLASES
I. — Definición
Ina cuola es cierta cantidad de mereaderia
determinada a la importación o a la exportación,
en el curso de un período fijo.
I1. — Sus Clases
Del concepto anterior, se desprende que exis-
ten dos clases de Cuotas:
Cuolas o Contingentes de Exportación; y
Cuotas o Contingentes de Importación.
CAPITULO SEGUNDO
CUOTAS O CONTINGENTES DE EXPORTACION
I. — Su naturaleza
Este sistema descansa sobre la base de un a-
cuerdo previo de los más importantes países ex-
portadores de una mercancia determinada, cuya
producción total excede a la capacidad de absor-
ción del meereado mundial.

amenazo con fijar una cuota a la importación d.
bembillas cléctricas japonesas. el Imperio ofreetó

Lilaterales de exportación . Fste caso es un reflejo

CAPITULO TERCERO
CUOTAS O CONTINGENTES DE
IMPORTACION
A. - GENERALIDADES
1. — Su Naturaleza
El sistema de cuotas o contingentes de im-
pot tarlón. radica. fundamentalmente, en la distri-
bución que. de la capacidad del mercado nacionel
para el consumo de tal mercancía, se hace entre
los países extranjeros que la producen.
II. — Su frecuencia
Al rovés de las cuolas de exportación. las de
importación no requieren un acuerdo universal
previo, ni es necesario que se trate de una mer-
cancia de soboe producción. Basta la acción bila-

-36-
teral; y, muchas veces, es suficiente la unilateral.
De aquí su frecuente aplicación.
III. — Su aplicación Universal
La Inión Industrial Argentina, por medio de
la Oficina de Estudios Económicos, publica en su
revista, bejo ef título: "Todos los países del mun-
do se detienden aduaneramente", síntesis quince-
nales de las últimas disposiciones sobre la mate-
ria. De Jos números de su revista — “Anales de la
Unión Industrial Argentina" — aparecidos du-
rante el presente año, extraigo los datos relativos
a las disposiciones sobre Contingentes y Licencias
de Importación. tomados sólo durante los ochlo
primeros meses de 1935.

Lima, 30 de octubre de 1935.
Señor Director de "El Comercio".
Ciudad.
Muy señor nuestro:
Ese prestigioso diario, en su edición
matinal de la fecha, comenta la infor-
mación. publicada ayer, sobre un a-
cuerdo adoptado por la Cámara de
Comercio del Cuzco, en lo relativo
a los efectos de la competencia japo-
nesa, que, con sobrada razón, juzga
intensamente perjudicial para la ma-
nufactura nacional de tejidos de algo-
dón.
El comentario en referencia con-
ceptúa ineficaz el sistema de cuotas.
aduciendo que los países que lo han
adoptado, tienden a reemplazarlo.
Esta institución tuvo conocimiento
directo, por telegrama del señor pre-
sidente de la Cámara de Comercio del
Cuzco, del mencionado acuerdo; y
puede asegurar que lo sustenta un
propósito de franco y decidido apo-
yo al régimen de Cuotas. y conside-
rándolo el único medio eficaz para
conjurar el problema.
Entendemos que la actitud de esa
Cámara obedece. precisamente. a la
necesidad de mantener contingenciados
los tejidos de algodón, previendo que
el 30 de noviembre próximo expira
el término señalado para su vigen
cia.
La apreciación de la Cámara en lo
relativo a la circunstancia de que las
Cuotas no han llenado su finalidad
con la amplitud deseada. se funda en
su tardía aplicación y en el elevado
margen concedido al Japón. A esto
cabe agregar que la reglamentación
del Decreto de 10 de mayo, excluye
los pedidos hechos hasta esta fecha.
Para reafirmar nuestro pensamien-
to, en lo que atañe a la eficiencia del
sistema de contingentes, nos bastaria
consignar la extensa relación de dis-
posiciones tomadas en ese sentido por
treintinueve paises, solamente en los
ocho primeros meses del presente año

Lima, October 19, 1936.
Subject: Further report concerning Japanese
Propaganda in Peru.
STRICTLY CUNFIDENTIAL
wm
ONMATION
1936
RICAN AFFAIRS
LATINA
 OF STATE
DEFARIME
894.20223/32
ilicut23
Ace
t e 23
a.
Division of
A01C
Jobt
FAR EASTERN ARS
NG DSK
The Monorable
0CT 30 1936
Department of State
3
Washington, D.C.
Referring to the Embassy's despatoh No. 4460
of March 17, 1936, regarding the increase of anti-
h
Japanese sentiment in Peru, I have the honor to
report that the press campaign has continued during
the past semester, centering principally on the
following topics: a) Japanese restaurants and bars,
stressing the poor quality of the drinks which are
stated to be synthetic and therefore injurious to
the health of the working classes; b) clandestine
immigration, notwithstanding the decree restricting
immigration

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
1mmigration; c) poor wages and bad treatment of
Peruvian peons on Japanese cotton plontations;
d) violation of the laws concerning the number of
Peruvians employed in Japanese establishments (80
percent law). Several clippings of srticles of this
1-15/ nature which have appeared in the last semester are
enclosed.
Presumably some of these attacks which have been
published in the more unimportant newspapers are the
result of an effort to secure financial support rom
Japanese sources on the promise that such unfriendly
propaganda would be discontinued. In this connection
it has been evident that advertising by Japanese firms
has increased very noticeably this year as compared
with previous years.
16/
On the other hand, occasional erticles in support
of the Japanese have appeared in some of the smaller
newspapers of Lima which are known not to be averse to
receiving financial contributions. For example, in
LA SANCION of July 28, 1936, an article signed "Romulo
16a/
Americano" was published, in which it was stated that
foreigners and not Peruvians are to blame for the cam-
paign against the Japanese. The point was also
stressed that the United States and European nations
are draining the natural resources of the country. A
17/
full page article, also in LA SANCION of July 28, de-
scribed the importance of the firm of Okada in the
Chancay Valley and closed with the comment that this
firm contributes to "the positive benefit of national
agriculture".

=== Page 113 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
agriculture". A contributed article in the July l7th
18/
number of EL UNIVERSAL referred to the en rgy of the
Japanese colony and its positive benefit for the econo-
mic development of Peru. The statement was also made
that the increased purchase by Japan of Peruvian
cotton has produced an increase in the price of this
commodity on world markets. The article went on to
say that in the writer's opinion the development of
the Japanese colony does not cause any danger to the
economic and political future of Peru, as the Japanese
show great respect for the laws of the country.
The activity of Japanese propaganda has concen-
trated particularly on sports and athletic events which
19/
have been used as a vehicle to serve the Japanese cause.
20/
It will be recalled that on the occasion of the Fourth
Centenary of Lima l st year, the Japanese colony pre-
sented a swimming pool to the City of Lima. This dona-
tion was given much publicity in conjunction with the
keen local interest in swimming, due to the recent good
performances of Peruvian amateur swimmers.
21/
More recently, on the occasion of the First cente-
nary of Callao (October 8, 1936), the Japanese colony
presented to the ity of Callao a grand-stand adjacent
to the municipal swimming pool.
The Japanese also seized the opportunity to play
on the feelings of the Peruvians - and magnified it to
the utmost - in connection with the reception prepared
for the returning Olympic team which withdrew from the
games at Berlin as a protest against a decision rendered
by

=== Page 114 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
by the "Fifa" (Federación Internacional Football Aso-
ciación), cancelling the football game at Berlin in
which the Peruvian team had beaten the Austrian
eleven (See Embassy's despatch No. 4670 of August 13,
1936). Extraordinary propaganda has been evident in
this connection, and the statement of the President of
the Japanese Central Society published in the newspa-
pers of September 17, 1936, of which a translation is
22/
enclosed, is worth reading as evidence of the methods
23/
used by the Japanese.
Another indication of Japanese
efforts to gain local good will was the picnic given on
24/
September 20, 1936, by the Japanese colony, at the
Athletic Field of the Japanese School, in honor of the
Peruvian team which had just returned from the lympic
gumes at Berlin. A further example  f Ju anese propa-
ganda was evident in the announcement in the newspapers
of August 30, 1936, that the Japanese Central Society
25/
has decided to collect funds to defray the expenses of
the Peruvian team which will porticipate in the Twelfth
Olympic games to be held in Tokyo. According to this
plan, every Japanese subject residing in Peru will con-
tribute one sol (25 cents) per month to this fund. The
Japanese Society estimates that in this manner it will
be able to collect from five to six thousand soles per
month, or about sixty thousand soles per annum, which,
by 1940 would amount to approximetely 240,000 soles,
(about $60,000).
A few months ago an oficer now in the Ministry of
Foreign Affairs in Lima, returned from Japan after
having

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
having served there three years as Peruvian consul
general. He states that he made  point of getting
into close touch with the Japanese and he found that
they have a lively interest in Peru. He said that
Chile, which has a climate that might be regarded as
better for Japanese, is more favorably inclined toward
them than is Peru, but despite this fect, Japanese
seem very much to prefer to come to Peru rather then
to go to Chile. He says that the Japanese have
special mental characteristics, p:rticularly whole-
hearted devotion to the interests of their nation;
that whereas citiz ns of the United States might leave
their country to earn more money, and Europeans in
search of territory, Japanese are more likely to go
abroad with the idea of serving their nation in some
manner. He mentioned the Japanese colony in the Amazon
region of Brazil and then mentioned the large Japanese
agricultural colony near the Huallaga River in Peru.
He seemed to have in mind that the connection between
the Brazilian and Peruvian colonies might not be so
distant as appears on the surface. He said that the
Japanese have published a number of articles concern-
ing Peru and that whereas a canal between the Pacific
Ocean and the Marañon River (leading into the Amazon)
has been regarded as merely fantastic in the Americas,
it had been a topic of conversation in Japan and the
Japanese had gone so far as to issue a publication on
the subject (although he didn't have a copy). When
the Japanese were asked if they were interested in
financing

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.87)
-6-
financing such a venture, they always replied - the
Embassy's informant sta ted - that they did not have
the money, but that as a possibility for the devel-
opment of the region, the proposal for such a canal
held great interest.
Respectfully yours,
Louis G. Dreyfus, Jr.,
Chargé d'Affaires
Ad interim.

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.99)
Enobsure No. 23 to Despatch No. 4765, October 19, 1936.
American Embassy, Lima,
TRANSLATION
Statement made by the President of the Central Japanese
Society on the occasion of the return to Peru of the
Athletes (Football Team, etc.) who took part in the
Olympic Games at Berlin.
THE CENTRAL JAPANESE SOCIETY GREETS HE PERUVIAN
OLYMPIC TEAM.
We are fascinated by their multiple
activities.
The next Olympics at Tokyo.
The Peruvians acted as real sportsmen. Great Interest
Shown.
Our homage in honor of the Peruvian Olympic Members.
At last the great day has a rived, awaited so im-
patiently. Today we are very happy, those of us who are
really true sportsmen; we are fascinated by the manifold
receptions tendered to the manly Peruvian sportsmen who
went to Berlin, full of faith and ideels, in order to win
their colors. They complied at every instant, but unfor-
tunately they were interrupted through agencies not to be
attributed to them; they worked whole-heartedly for their
colors nd in honor of Peru.
Full of Enthusiasms.
After a long trip it was anticipated that they would
show fatigue and that they would have been affected
morally as a consequence of the great and unexpressable
injustice to which they had been subject; but it has
proved otherwise as they have disembarked agile and
vigorous, full of enthusiasm, thereby proving induscutibly
their excellent quadifications as sportsmen and their
elevated spirit and iron will.
Love

=== Page 155 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
Love for Sports.
There is a reason why many well-known persons assert
that the progress and development of a Nation is based
fundamentally on the development of its sportsmanlike
qualities, due to the intimate relation existing between
the physical and the moral factors in the individual;
therefore, as a question f patriotism, these sports
slould be developed, taking care to cultivate good sports-
anship, facilitating full support and more particularly
in favor of the youth who should learn to love sports.
The Japanese Colony.
The Japanese colony in Peru, whose members are sin-
cerely fond of the country, have followed constantly and
with great interest every act that pertains to its pro-
gress and general welfare; and it is noted with partic-
ularly great enthusiasm the first and the second victor-
ies of the Peruvian footbell team, sharin: them as though
they had been won by Japanese. .or this reason we have
extended to them our sympathy and we have considered, as
an attitude against us, the unjust decision against that
famous team - an aggregation which has shown its power
and knowledge of the game, acting with a sportsmanship
which will remain as a proud moment for Peruvian sports,
and which has merited the congratulations for their
patriotic efforts.
Day of Jubilee
Today we also join in this jubilee. For this reason
we accompany these Peruvian sportsmen in their just cele-
bration and we have the most ardent hopes for their con-
tinued success and progress along lines of physicel
prowess

=== Page 156 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
prowess and good sportsmenship of which they have given
such a good account at the Olympic games in Berlin. Let
us hope that the deceptions which they suffered there may
prove to bo a motive to develop their fighting spirit and
that it may serve as an experience to prove their good
qualities when they meet opponents in the future.
The Next Olympic Games.
The next Olympic games will be held at Tokyo where
those countries which met at Berlin will assemble again.
Every effort will be made to assure the maximum of jus-
tice and correct action, thereby giving the Peruvian
sportsmen a wonderful opportunity to repeat their magni-
ficent record, reaffirming their qualifications and
exceptional aptitude which they displayed against ad-
verse factors such as climate, environment, absence from
the family circle and from friends, and the natural
lassitude of a tiresome trip; and yet they excelled dur-
ing the games.
They will give Peru a good name.
We have the greatest faith that this will occur; and
we feel sure, therefore, that the name of Feru will fig-
ure prominently in the forthcoming Olympic games. We are
sure that their reputation as good pleyers will be main-
tained and that they will conduct themselves on a plane
which corresponds to these facts.
R. Takahashi,
President of the Central Japanese
Society.

Enclosure 1 to despatch No. 4983 of February 26, 1937 from
the Embassy at Lima.
JAPANES: COLOLIZATION
Lima, Peru.....(AP) The Peruvian epublie is one among the
few nations of the world which having a small population es
compared with the size of their territory whose Government
has been forced to take drastic steps to limit considerably
immigration. at first sight this perhaps seems senseless but
the Government undoubtedly believed it wiser to establish
r gulations of general character not vishin: to pass a legis-
lation prohibiting certain immigi tion, a f t that would
affect nly one nation with which it always maintained very
friendly r lations. The real cause of thie measure was the
considerably Japanese i migration to Peru, which instead of
being a favo ab e tct r tor the progress, aevelopment and
welfare of the country was frankly a ne ative ne in  ery
sense.
Forein colonies in Peru represented as of June 1, 1936,
according to officiul fi ures 43.317 indivi uals duly registered
at the aliens Department of the Interior Ministry. of these
22.560 were Japs who formed the largest foreign group living
in this netion and representing a little more than 5og of the
aliens. ut these 22.560 individuals, men, women and c ildren
never had a favorable influence on Peru's existence. Americans
who have made censiderable money investments mostly connected
with the mining industry; Italians who are he owners of the
largest Bank o he nation and ritishers who have been con-
nected with Peru since th first days of its independent life
have been very important factors for t: e prosperity ano progress
that Peru has attained and it is  akin prese tly with almost
unb-lievable sveed. Many of these aliens married Peruvian
girls en if married women of their own nationality they never
objected their children to become Peruvian citizens if born
in this country. Their commereial houses, inoustriel concerns,
mining plants, banks and ectories have large staffs of P ruvian
employees and the yearly profits are reinvested here through
the enlargement of their various enterprises or de osited in
the local bun s.
But tnis is not the case with Jupanese immigrants. Their
principal chars teristic is their selfish and "nationalistic"
spirit. They do not mix with P ruvians and most of their
personal or social cont: cts are limited to baseball games and
athletic tournaments. They do not marry Peruvien women and
report goes on that single Japs are married in most of the
cases to pic ure-brides which they choose from sets of photo-
graphs received from their native country when they instruct
their relatives in Jepan to look for a wife for them.
Japanese children born in Peru never become Peruvian
citizens. First of all they attend Japanese schools of which
there are scores all over the country. The teachers are
Japanese who educate the children according to the standards
observed in any school of any community of the Rising Sun
Empire, as Japan is called sometimes. It is not infrequent
to hear

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
to hear wiile passing in front of the local Japanese school
infantile voices shouting loud and enthusiastic "banzais"
or see them bowing deeply to Emperor Hirohito's picture.
Consequently children are trained to be 1o0% Japanese
citizens as in their teaching there is an absolute lack of
Peruvian ideals or  nowleage of the country where they were
born.
Regarding other life aspects it shoula be pointed out
that the Japenese merchant, carpenter, constructor, far er,
etc. rinds alweys some means to evade rules forcin: foreign
concerns t hire at least an 8o? Peruvien staff so most of
the money to be paid in salaries and spent in the local
market does not return to Peruvian hands. in the other
side the cheap and low standard of life of Japanese colonists
makes the latter he greatest competitors of Peruvian workers
or foreign merchants. Japanese live almost in seclusion and
despite their large umber in Lima very few are seen in the
streets in holidays, except on their official "fiesta" --
Hirohito's pirthday when they gather at the grounds of the
local Japanese School to hold athletic meetings after the
customary speech-making by p. ominent  mbers of the Colony.
Japanese carpenters, bricklayers, farmers, etc., having less
needs than Peruvians charge lower prices and make whet is
looked as an unfair competition to national workers. hen
after several years of rivations they amass a little capital
the immigrants go back to their native country carrying it
with them provided they did not remit it regularly every year
to Japan through local Japenese commercial houses which act
for them as Ban s.
In order to deal with these conditions the Peruvian ov-
ernment decreed on June 1936, that thereafter all Foreign
Immigration should be subjected undiscriminately to a quota
of two-per-thousand on a basis of 16.000 individuals or
each nationality. The decree also stated that foreigners
leaving the country should declare before departing their
intention to come back but if during their absence the
16.000 individual quota is exhausted they cannot return to
Peru. As Japanese for the only colony exceeding the 16.000
individuals figure these rules mean that no more immigrants
of their nationality are to be allowed to come to Peru, and
that those going away wi ll not be able to come back unless
the colony dwindles under the above limit. These rules are
not to be applied to any other of foreign colonies. According
to the official statistics as of June lst, l936 the nationalities
exceeding 1,000 individuals were besides the Japanese, the
following: Chinese: 6.900; Italians, 5852; British, 230l;
Germans 2242; Americans 2229; Spanish, 1584, and Chilean 1654.
Japanese are the only ones exceeding the 16.000 quota.
PERUVIAN JAPANESE COMMERCIAL RELATIONS.
Lima, Peru....(AP) In 1929 Peru signed a Trade Treaty with
Japan granting each other the "most favored nation" clause and
apparently then nobody had the least idea that the agreement
some years later would affect deeply one of the most importent
Peruvian industries in which American and other foreign capitals
are conspicuously represented. When Japan through the devaluation
of the yen

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.95)
-3-
of the yen and on account of its low cost production started
to dump the world with its products the Peruvian Textile Industry
felt its effects and its prosperity started to dwindle, the
result being that the capital was not only menaced but also
a great number of workers and white-collar men.
Therefore Peru notified Japan the abrogation of the
Treaty to be effective on October 5th, 1935, and when the
agreement ceased to exist the Finance Department decreed the
establishment of quotas for the importation of cotton and
other textile fabrics.
This decree was also intended to free the national
textile industry not only of the Japanese competition but
also of others such as the British. A Trade Treaty signed
between Peru and Great Britain on October 6th, 1936 disposed
of the problem. But so far commerciel relations wit Janan
remain in the state.
When on account of its special conditions Japan was able
3
to produce goods cheaper than other countries, reru by first
time realized the real meaning of the abro ated Treaty.
5
Japanese imports climbed tremendously as years passed while
Peruvian exports to Japan did not increase. Newspaper comments
11
on this peculiar situation described the increase of the
0
Nipponese trade in this country as a "commercial invasion" and
it can be safely stated that these conditions combined with the
2
shy, selfish life that Japanese carry on in Peru are responsible
for the Immgiration restrictions decreed last year by the
12
Peruvian Government.

In despatch no. 245 of JanuamyE1

An 1mpressive collection of news-
paper clippings is transmitted with the
despatch, which, from a cursory reading,
demonstrate that Peruvian opinion is
considerably aroused.
The Japanese immigrant enters Peru
as a cheap farm hand and through his
lower standard of living, his tenacity,
his solidarity with other Japanese who
have preceded him, and his capacity for
saving, graduates into a small crafts-
man, then acquires property, and finally
branches out as an owner of a store.
Eventually some may enter the manufacturing
field and return to agricultural pursuits
"not as laborers but as landholders".
The following statistics taken from

1937, are impressive:

POLITICAL REPORT
C O N F I D E N T I A L
AERTCAN CONSULATE PNRAL,
Callao-Lima, Peru,
January7, 1938.
SUBJECT: Anti-Japanese Feeling in Peru
The Honorable
The Amrican Anbassador to Peru,
Lima.
Sir:
I heve the honor to transmit herewith full trans-
lations of Now Year's salutations b: prominent Japanese
husiness men of Lima. These addresses appeared in the
leading newsptpers of this city on January 1st and 4th
respectively. They a e worthy o careful reading be-
cause each of them is a brief for the defense and every
line in each is either a denial of an allegation or a
plea in mitiation.
For some three or four years past a gradurlly in-
creasing pressure of anti-Japanese feeling has been mak-
ing itself felt in Peru. This has sharply increased
since the present Japanese military action in China bean
and it is apperent that the leader of the Japanese com-
munity in Peru and that community as a whole have sudden-
ly b come aware of it.
Aside from antastic allegations of military organ-
ization and preperation the most serious charge a ainst
Jepunese immigrants in Peru is that they constitute a
state within a state, & self-continuing roup of unassimil-
able end permanent aliens. Chinese men in Peru marry or
consort with Peruvian women of the lower classes an raise
families which gradually merge into the mass of the popu-
lation. These Jepanese men rarely do. They have picture
brides brought over, their children are purely Japanese
in blood, go to Japanese schools, are inculcated with
Japanese culture, racial pride and reverence for the
Emperor, and they marry exclusively within the Japanese
community.
Now Mr. T. Kurotobi, an interesting personality, who
from a contracted field hand on a sugar plantation has
risen to be a wealthy merchant and landowner and is now
President of the Central Japanese Society of Peru, asserts
that there are only 13,000 Japanese in Peru. He reaches
this conclusion by asserting that his children, and, by
inference, all other children born in Peru of Japanese
parents, are doubly Peruvian, both by Peruvian law and
by affection for the land of their birth. This, at one
whack reduces the number of Japanese in the country from
thirty or thirty-five thousand to thirteen thousand and
also vastly increases the number of Peruvian employees
working

=== Page 178 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
working in Japanese enterprises as all children working
in their parents shops or stores are by this new system
to be counted as reruvians. Just u few years ago any
suggestion of this kind would have been repudiated in-
dignantly by almost any Japanese. If this principle is
to prevail the Jepenese uestion in Peru will be solved
in one generation - unfortunately, nobouy believes that
Peruvian-born Japanese are Peruvian in anything except
a technical sense.
The second ulleation which r. urotobi denies is
that Japanese immigrants displace Peruvian working people.
He states thet there were no Peruvian sellers of cold
drinks or shaved ice, no Peruvian barbers, operators of
cafes nor storekeepers when the Ja enese went into these
lines of business eni that as Peruvians entered them the
Japanese have generously stepped aside and lefi the field
to their native conpetitors. The popular belief is that
by their herd work, long hours, low scale of living and
mututl aid they monopolize any business they choose and
that it is impossible for Peruvians to co pete with ther.
a third allegation which r. Iurotobi attacks is
that J tnese send or take all thoir money back to J: pan.
He asserts th t in 35 years he has never returned to Japan
and that all the money he hos made in eru is invested in
Peru. That thi: is true in the case of Mr. Kurotobi
everyooay dmits. He is a large landowner, a generous
giver to Peruvian cherities and admittedly feels a sin-
cere affection for th: land where he hes done so well.
But in popular opinion he is the only one of whom this
is true - all other J. panese clin together, patronize
each otner, use Jn tnese products and use Peru only as
a place to make money and not to spend any. In this
popular opinion there is undoubtedly a gooa deal of
exaggeration. Japanese own many enterprises of importance
in Peru and these have required the investment of capital.
The idea that the Japanese residents in Peru are
organized militarily and are located strategically in
accordance with the plans of some higher command is rether
effectively de olished by r. urotobi's s atement regard-
ing the number of Japenese residing in each of the local-
ities he mentions. The distribution is exactly that of
poor people seeking those places where they can make a
better living. Nevertheless all Peruvians know that all
these thousands of Japanese scattered over the country
are solidly loyal to the land of their birth and that
whenever officers of the Emperor desire to obtain infor-
mation of a military character about Peru they have com-
pletely pe rfect means at their command.
The number of Peruvians which Mr. Kurotobi says are
employed by Japanese bakers, barbers and storekeepers is
given out as a contradiction of the prevailing idea that
Japanese employ only Japanese. Evidently, however, he is
counting all Peruvian-born children of Japanese parents
as Peruvians. In this he is justified by Peruvian law but
not by the opinion of the man-in-the-street in Lima, who
believe

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
believe that Japanese children born in Peru, educated in
Japanese schools, taught Japanese history and brought up
in loyalty to the Emperor are exclusively Japenese and
asserts that on one ever heard of one of these eruvian-
born Japanese serving in the Peruvian Army or Navy.
Mr. Rinuemon Takahashi, as President of a Chamber
of Commerce, replies to a different class of allegations.
He stresses the fact that mutual aid to Japanese engaged
in business is that of person to person and that there is
no organization which provides Japanese with funds for
such purposes. There is a belief, no doubt erroneous,
but widespread, and popular all the same, that the Sentral
Japanese Society and even the Japenese consulate provide
conmunity or governmental funds to establish Japenese
in business and to k ep them going in times of difficulty.
He s ys that Japanese business men are eager to inaugurate
industries in P ru which will ive employment to natives
and develop the country. He stresses the obvious fact
that Peru is a raw material ccuntry while Japan is in-
dustrial and tat the t.o ere thus economically comple-
mentary. His suggestion that p ru could double its
present cotton crop a sell it all in Jupan une then
would be supplyin only 20 percent of the demand will
undoubtedly make an impression here for Peuvians like
to row and sell cotton. Unfortunetely tic statement
glides around the fact that Peruvitn cotton is fine in
quality and high in price while the Japanese demand is
principally for the cheapest grades of cotton. The
reference to the fact that Jepan provides a market for
200 million yen worth of wool was very apropos for at
the moment Peru i: very seriously intent on increasing
its wool prouuction.
Mr. Takahashi's suggestion that the Japanese chamler
of commerce is ready to assist in financing the trip of a
commission of Peruvian business men to visit Japan ought
to result in a first class junket but it is probable that
such a scheme will meet with governnental discouragement.
Thinking people in Peru both in and out of the Govern-
ment circles are profoundly disturbed b the uncontrollable
actions of the Japanese military and naval leaders in China.
The question "What if the Japanese Admiralty should decide
that Peru offers a good field for Japanese expansion," has
suddenly presented itself to their minds. It was this
idea which caused "La Cronica" of Lima, a newspaper of Lima,
a newspaper not always too pro-American in its utterances,
to say in a recent editorial that the armed power of the
United States is the steel door protecting the liberties
of all America and that if it were ever battered down by
gunfire, it would be a sad day for Latin America.
The Japanese immigrants living in Peru are, without
exception, working people some of whom, by grim determina-
tion and desperate efforts, heve built up businesses and
acquired competences and in some cases, wealth. It is
to be presumed that they secretly disapprove of the actions
of the military caste now taking place in China as much as
the business

=== Page 180 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
the business classes in Japan are said to disapprove.
Naturelly they cannot express such disapproval of their
own country's action while in a foreign environment but
they are very keen to accentuete ane emphasize other
facets of Japanese character. An opportunity for such
expression was presented by the arrival of the Japanese
tenor, Fujiwara, on January 7, 1938. On the day previous
the Japanese Association of Commerce and Industry, the
Central Japanese Society and twenty-four of the leading
Japanese business firms took entire pages in the morning
newspapers to display their cordial Welcome to "The
Ambassador of Japanese Art". Two concerts only were to
be iven but a third was later arranged by the Peru-
Japanese Culturl Society, members of the Japanese colony
taking practically all the lower boxes. A fourth concort
in farewell, of which the entire proceeds wele to be
handed over to Peruvian charities, vas iven on Sunday,
Januery l6th, and all these concerts were advertised in
the newspapers by apanese business firms. Despite all
this effort little enthusiasm was aroused ana it is re-
ported that at every performance more than half the
audience was Japanese and the da ily papers inored the
event in their news colunns.
Tnat the Japenese in Peru are conscious of & rising
tide of feelin egeinst them ad that they are much dis-
turbed by the insecurity of their position is palpably

ood wishes that the notatle national and ecenomic pros-

aoes not lso preveil in our country. Refore the unchecked

to themselves and to the hos itable land which ave them
birth.

of Peru, they are the personal qualifications deriving from
my intense affection for this country which in some form

"Japanese I migrants end Peruvian Agriculture.

up until 1922 when some 33 contin ents landing totalled about

"Referring first to the Japanese field hands I must

al though by being better they benefit him not at all but

in small quantities, often sacrificing legitimate profits

"There are other occupations to which the Japanese

"The number of bakeries run by Jepanese in Lima is 24;
they ive employment to 15o Peruvian workers and operate

and try always to ive the best possible service to their

"The business of storekeeping, at least in the way
in which it is established, hus peculiar characteristics
in accordance with the varying requirements of the dif-
ferent sections of the city where the shops are located.
In years gone by retail trade in this class of articles
was limited to certain central streets; even in these each
shop specialized in the sale of only certain articles and
all this commerce was in th hands of foreigners. Would-be
buyers then had to take the trouble to visit numbers of
different stores generally far from their homes in order to
effect their purchases. As for prices there were great
differences not only between those asked in one shop and
another but even in those asked of different customers in
the same shop. Knowing  how to buy' was an indispensable
art if one would avoid paying too much. Little by little
all of these inconveniences have been swept away by the
establishment of the Japanese bazaars. Located in differ-
ent quarters of the city and its suburbs every one of them
has

=== Page 187 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
has in stock, as fer as possible, everything needed by
dwellers in its neighborhood, being most plentiful those
goods which correspond to the buyint po er of the customers.
Arbitrarily fixed prices huve been succeeded by a uniform
system of prices calculated on the basis of minimum profits
in accordance with the commereial principle that the
smallest purtial profits bring the greatest volume of trade
and therefore bring in the largest total profits as well as
the most benefit to the buyer who gets more goods for his
money. Another of the benefits brought by the Japanese
bazaars is the introduction of Japanese merchandise which
because of their good quality and lov prices have merited
the frank acceptance of the public.
"I must note here reterr n directly to iruvian manu-
factures that they are sou ht by .apanese merchents and
offered to their customers ordinarily at insignificent
profits with the object of making known their good qual-
ities and contrihutin: to the development of the industries
of this country.

have had :nd heve on the life of Lima has been brought

which formerly brought happiness only to e privileged group
of children of more or less wealthy families this year
brought joy to the entire infantile population owing to the
vide assortment of toys offered for le in the bazars at
prices low enou:n to be in the reech of even the poorest
eople. As a numerical reference I vill say thet there
are 77 bazaars in existence which ive employment to 380
persons, of whom 320 are Peruvians and 60 Japenese. It
is plain then that in all the tctivities in which my
countrymen ontered they heve aone so in a comprenensive
and nonest spirit ithout narrow egotism and with the

LA PR NSA
January 4, 1938.
Lima, Peru.
" OUR MISSION
IN THA PHOM TIO: OF
RUVIAN-JAPANESE ECC: OMIC RELATIONS.
By
Rinuemon Takahashi,
President
of the
JAPANLS ASSOCIATTON OF COMMERCE ANL INDUSTRY.
At the dawn of the New Year it is extremely pleasant
for me to express to all Peruvians, in the name of the
Japanese Association of Commerce and Industry, the good
wishes that we entertain for the greatest possible prosperity
of this generous land in the year now beginning and our
heartiest co: gratulations on the gigantic development to be
seen in all circles of national activity as much from the
point of view of education and culture as in industrial and
economic aspects. The year 1937 has esta lished a record
in the rising prosperity of Peru and our hope is that this
record may be surpa: sed in the year now be inning.
Institutional Objectves
The Japanese Association of Commerce and Inuustry was
founded in the year 1935 not only with the intent of tight-
ening the ties and relations of the Japanese dedicated to
commerce, industry and a riculture in this country but also
with the objection of collaborating with Peruvian elements
to the end of establishing closer economic links between
our countries by means of reciprocal advertising of natural
and industrial products, facilitating transactions and cultivat-
ing merkets as well as of new industries which might be estab-
lished in Peru.
This country, in short, possesses a vast territory, hav-
ing incalculable natural resources, which causes a constantly
increasing agricultural, mineral and pastoral production hut
at the same time, leaves hidden inexhaustible but unexploited
resources in conditions of more or less accessibility the
development of which would contribute powerfully to the greater
prosperity of the nation.
We try to collaborate in this sense with the Peruvian
element in the creation of real walth both of the industrial
and the natural sorts broadcasting their qualities not only
in Japan but in all the latitutdes of the world and promoting
in this manner the best industrial development.
"Industrial and Commercial Relations between Peru and Japan.
"It is pleasing for us to state that in this country thousands
of Japanese devote themselves to different occupations and pro-
fessions in constant harmony with Peruvians sharing their joys
and their

=== Page 189 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
and their sorrows in cordiality and sympathy. We believe
that this cordiality and sympethy ought to be expanded and
accentuate through increased collaboration which will
prive mutually aivantageous.
"The members of this association are, in effect, whole-
sale merchants on a large scale and have a lovely interest
not only in the sale of imported articles but also in that
of those manufactured in Peru and in the exportation of the
country's natural products. From this point of view it may
be said that relations between Japanese merchants and
Peruvian producers are so close that between them both there
exists an effective interdependence for at present the quan-
tity and value of Peruvian products handled and sold by
Japanese merchants is very appreciable. Meanvhile Peruvian
producers highly esteem my countrymen vho dedicate them-
selves with enthusiasm to advertising and selling their
products.
"So then it is very gretifying for us to note that
however much the national wealth and production of Peru
increase the more fortunate the Japanese merchants will be,
while, reciprocally, the prosperity of the letter contributes
to the industrial development of the nation stimulating pro-
uction so that the bonds between the two parties are con-
stantly ti. htened.
"For the rest, the Japanese merchants do not have the
slightest intention of displacing the Peruvian element but,
very much on the contrary, intend to collaborate with it
and lend their aid to the development of the national economy
and wealth. They are always inspired by high senti ents of
solidarity and responsibility which keeps them serving at
every moment with honesty tryin to develop the greatest
volume of sales with the smallest profit per sale and ad-
hering inflexibly to the legal and moral rules coverning
commerce.
"We should declare in passing to avoid current misunder-
standings that there exist certain shops that with names and
appearances similar to those of Japanese stores continue to
exploit the credulity of the public by unspeakably disloyal
competition. In this respect it is our desire to make things
clear to avoid that by our silence the frauds should accen-
tuate themselves.
"Japanese Industrials.
"Among the members of this association figure some manu-
facturers who, day after day, study the possibilities of
importing new models and machines for use in this country
following with close attention the industrial progress of
Japan. They consider necessary the establishment in Peru
of diverse industries capable of serving the necessities
of the inhabitants with which object they constantly study
the possibilities of initiating new industries. Our Asso-
ciation will take pleasure in putting any one interested
in questions of this sort in contact with the persons to
whom I have referred.
"Japanese

=== Page 190 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
"Japanese Farmers.
"In the field of proauction agriculture is perhaps the
most noble activity as being that lost closely bound up in
the traditions of the Peruvian race. In this aspect I am
glad to say that the Japanese colony contains many excellent
farmers who strive methodically to obtain the highest pro-
duction both in quality and quantity. Some members of this
Association devote themselves by preference to the cultiva-
tion of cotton. Japanese planters, keeping in mind that
this fiber constitutes the principal aericultural export
of Peru, have been striving: for the betterment and augmenta-
tion of the c op with a view t, acquiring the best accept-
ance in world markets.
"The Spirit of the Japanese Residents.
"Not all the japanese immigrants settled in this country
who have attained success of more or less importance have
followed the same line of activity. Some have reached the
positions they hold counting only the moral capital of
their industry and enthusiasm while others initiated their
activities with capital brought from Japen. But the motive
of all the immigrants was identical; they came with the pur-
pose of conquering a better future and they found here a
generous and privileged country. The condition in which they
now find themselves is due to their respective efforts and
to the mutual aid iven in moments of necessity by person
to person without the economic intervention of any central
organization and, much less, without the aid of the Govern-
ment of my country as some persons assert lightly and with-
out round. But if the Japanese colony in Peru has anything
of which it may justly be proud this something will certainly
be its sentiments of ratitude. Its members never forget
the tranquil and ha py hours passed here in union with
their families and seek always to find the best forms of
repaying the traditional hospitality of the Peruvian people.
"Judging matters in accordance with what I have just said
it appears that the intimate bond of interest between Japanese
merchants and industrialists and those of Peru is based prin-
cipally upon the affectionate sentiments which my country-
men have for this country.
"A manifestation of the said sentiments, to which I may
refer because it is connected with commerce, lies in the low
prices at which they (Jepanese merchants) sell merchendise
of good quality and great popularity. They repay in this
manner the patronage of the public even though it is true
that in many cases they are able to quote such prices only
because of the self imposed sobriety of their own lives in
the conviction that beneficial self-sacrifice is above all.
They toil assiduously and punctually from morning until
night serving the public always not only with the greatest
honesty but also in the best spirit manifesting in every
moment that they are content to obtain larger volume of
sales although it is with the smallest profit. This "Good
and cheap" services proves indubitably beneficial for the
mass of Peruvian consumers and most of all for the poorest.
It is

=== Page 191 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
It is good for both sellers and buyers that the commerce
of the Japanese should continue to maintain itself within
this rule of favorine the  ublic.
"Peruvian Employees in Japanese nterprises.
"With respect to Peruvian-Japanese collaboration which
our Association, and, in general, all the colony to which
I belong, propose it is proper that I refer now to the bond
that exists between Japanese employers and Peruvian employees.
"The members of this Association vho direct the most
important enteryrises of the Japenese Colony employ from
single individuals up to hundreds of workmen and employees
who are not Japanese. I may assert that the workers have
always expressed themselves as leased with the treatment
which they receive and with the spirit which animates the
owners and directors of the enterprises whose relations
with the personnel under their orders have nothing of the
frigidity commonly existing between 'capitalist and toiler?
but rather are as if they were partners in the business
with the traditional Japanese spirit of mutual cooperation
and mutual respect for the persc na ity of ech.
"Commerce between Peru and Japan.
"In the economic organism of the present-day world the
progress of a state often depends upon the development of
international trade. A country like Peru where natural re-
sources abound, should try to export raw materials while a
country like Japan, very industrialized, has to try to sell
its manufactures in foreign markets. In this vay commerce
between the two countries benefits both equally. Peru actual-
ly exports the majority of the raw materials that it produces
except a small proportion used by national industry; Japan,
for its part, sustains its factories with materials imported
fro countries enjoying natural riches. It is obvious that
both nations are in a position to expand their commercial
interchange. It is thus natural that every day the commer-
cial relations between Peru and Japan should be closer.
For these reasons the exports of Peru to Japan have increased
in 1936 to more than S/o. 14,000,000, which represents a
figure equal to twice the value of Peruvian imports from
Japan in the seme year so that, in accordance with the com-
mercial balance, the benefit to Peru is unquestionable.
"One commits a lamentable error in lightly accepting
the thesis that Japan exports much and buys little. Unfor-
tunately my co ntry lacks natural resources, its raw materials
being supplied by foreign countries. In cotton alone, the
manufacture of which is the most important Japanese industry,
imports have been made auring the past year to the value of
850 million yen. Wool to the value of 200 million yen was
imported. The articles made of these materials were not
necessarily exported in their entirety for the majority
were consumed in Japan only being shipped abroad the surplus
the value of which could not come to more than half the
value of the cotton and wool imported.
"Japan is interested in buying raw materials from the
South American countries among which may be especially
mentioned Argentina, Brazil and Chile from which the total
purchases are larger each year.
The prinoipal

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
"The principal Peruvian product exportable to Japan
is cotton, a rew material which the Japenese textile in-
dustry aosorbs, the value of the importations exceeding,
as I have said, 850 million yen, a sum ten times greater
than the value of the entire cotton crop of Peru. This
means that even if Peru should double its cotton production
and this was entirely exported to Japan this country could
then only satisfy twenty percent of the Jepanese consumption.
"Peru, nevertheless, has many other materials, such
as wool and diverse minerals, which can be exported to
Japan.
"Understanding the metter thus it is re rettable that
commercial relations between our two countries are not closer
and that their definite economic vinculation is postponed
despite the Pacific Ocean which bathes and unites the
coasts of both countries facilitating transportation. This
is due, undoubtedly, to the shortness of the time that has
elapsed since the establishment of commercial connections
as the intimate nature of commercial relations requires
wide knowledge just as do relations between persons.
Economic Friendship between Peru and Japan.
"It is necessary in order to tighten the economic rela-
tions between our two countries that we try to acquire this
ample knowledge in reciprocal form, expressing sincerely
that which each  rty desires in relation to the quality,
the quantity, the rice, the style, etc., of very article
be it raw material or manufectured oods. To this end it
would be advisable to organize an economic mission.
"Some years back Japan exchanged economic missions with
Brazil and recently Chile sent a mission of the same sort
made up of outstanding personalities of different sectors
of production and commerce to exchange opinions in a frank
and direct form with official and private elements upon
commerce, maritime transportation, culture, etc., with bene-
fit to both countries in consequence of the impuise given
to economic relations. It is because of the success obtain-
ed then that we declare that it is indisnensable that a
Peruvian mission of this class be sent to my country with
the object of expanding and deepenin mutual acquaintance
and understanding seeing that our friendly relations are
of the oldest date among the South American countries. This
Association will leave nothing undone toward collaborating
with Peruvian elements in an enterprise of this nature.
"There are many roads by which the commerce and industry
of this country may be conducted to a higher and more pros-
perous level, strenghtening at the same tie the economic
and friendly relations that unite it to my country. In
these roads the Association over which I preside is always
present, cooperating without stint and ever seeking the
utmost harmony with the population in general and with
Peruvian merchants and manufacturers in particular.
"In this

=== Page 193 === (confidence 0.96)
-6-
"In this happy opportunity of reetiny the New Yeer
of 1938, I offer my felicitations and my best wishes for
the weli being and prosperity of Peru, attained and
maintained in order and peace.
"Lima, (Peru) January 3, 1938."

h)—La depreciación monetaria.

pón suspendía el patrón de oro, de-
valuando el yen; y el 1o. de julio
do 1932 establecia el control de cam-
bios. De esta manera el Yen, que

oro. Es clerto que muchos otro
paiscs han recortado el valor de sus
divisas.
)—La modicidad de los salarias.

bajo es sensiblemente mayor que
en los paises occidentales. Tomemos

a a 9.93; y en la tintoreria y a.
aabado, alcanza 10.57. (Los minutos
 calculan como decimales de La
hora. Los datos corresponden a di-
slembre de 1984.)

Clippings from La Noche, Lima October 29, 1937.
Ei Cbi/me
del.Dla
FILANTROPIA ASIATICA
El señor don H. Ikeyama, Director del diario ja-
ponés "Perú Jiho", es el firmante de unos muy intere-
santes artículos en castellano en los cuales se trata de
demostrar las incontables ventajas que se derivan pa-
ra nosotros de la crecienie penetración japonesa.
El señor Ikeyama firma esos artículos. Suponemos
que también los escribe.
Pero ese es otro cuento.
Según el ilustrado periodista nipón, ellos, los nipo.
nes, han comprometido la gratitud de todos los perua-
nos. Con su abnegación, con su desprendimiento, con su
laboriosidad, con su filantropía.
Si, con su filantropía
Porque, como puede leerse en uno de sus últimos
artículos, hay empresas japonesas — se refiere a la "Pe
rú Takushoku Kumiai — que no aspiran a obtener otras
utilidades que contribuir, con su esfuerzo "al mayor de.
sarrollo de la riqueza peruana".
Qué conmovedor! Y qué emocionante!
Ya es hora de que abramos los ojos y nos conven-
zamos de que los japoneses están animados, con respec-
to a nosotros, de las mejores intenciones.
Cuando ellos desplazan a los yanaconas y cam-
pesinos peruanos — por ejemplo en el valle de Chan.
cay — no lo hacen siguiendo un plan cuidadoso de pene-
tración, sino, solamente, para impulsar el desarrollo del
Perú.
Cuando burlan las leyes del trabajo que fijan el
porcentaje mínimo de personal peruano, lo hacen para in-
crementar la riqueza del país, aun contra nuestra voluntad,
e incluso colocándose al margen de la ley.
Cuando intentan sobornar a los funcionarios pe-
ruanos, es sólo para probar su honestidad y no porque en
realidad tengan el propósito de inducirlos a la inmorali-
dad.
Cuando niegan el trabajo a los peruanos, y ocu.
pan, únicamente, a los carpinteros japoneses, a los pin-
tores japoneses, a los gasfiteros japoneses, a los hojalate.
ros japoneses, a los vidrieros japoneses, etc., es que quie
ren darnos lecciones prácticas de solidaridad.
Proceden así contra su voluntad. Para favorecer-
nos. Para ayudarnos. Y hasta exponiéndose a parecer 
nemigos de la prosperidad y del bienestar de los perua-
Es la forma más elevada, más pura, y más nobl
filantropía.
Filantropía nipona.
LUCK.

LOS INMIGRANTES JAPONESES Y LA AGRICULTURA PERUANA
Ahora, bien, durante el tiempo que he trabajado como jornalero agri-
estrictos cumplidores de sus compromisos y realizaban siempre su trabajo
con efectiva conciencia de su responsabilidad. Radica principalmente en es-
propietario de terrenos, he estado siempre, en alguna forma, vinculado & la
cola y durante todos los anos posteriores hasta hoy, en que soy, a mi vez,
convirtiendose aigunos de ellos de simples trabajadores a domicilio en p
tas condiciones personales el que consiguieran progresar en sus actividades
agricultuia peruana, en la que cimenté la base de la fortuna que logré
despues incrementar en el comercio. He sido testigo del denodado esfuerzo
de mis compatriotas dedicados aqui a faenas agricolas desde que comenza-
quenos industriales.

Estoy seguro que todos los peruanos que han celebra o contratos de
arrendamientos de tierras con agricultores japoneses, o los han emplea-
do como braceros en sus fundos, apoyaran sinceramente mis palabras, cdán-
doles el singular valor de sus propias experiencias.
Ahora bien, los japoneses que llegamos al Peru hasta 1922, encontra-
mos la agricultura de la costa necesitada de brazos. Al irse solucionando
paulatinamente este problema, muchos optamos por dedicarnos a otras ac-
tividades. En su mayoria se establecieron en Lima, dedicandose a la ven-
ta de emolientes, refrescos, hielo raspado, etc.; pero en cuanto los peruanos
comenzaron a dedicarse a estas actividades, ellos convergieron hacia otros
oficios y pequeñas industrias, pues el proposito del japonés es no ser com-
petidor en ninguna industria del peruano, pues reconocen la preferencia
que todo nacional debe tener en su propia tierra. aunque reconozca el es-
piritu ampliamente liberal de la Constitucion del Peru. que trata por igua!
a peruanos y extranjeros en una solidaridad fruto de la proverbial genero-
sidad de esta hospitalaria República, siendo oportuno señalar que en la
actualidad ningún compatriota mio se ocupa en la venta ambilante e
esta clase de articulos.
Las industrias a que mayormente se dedicaron pasada esa primera eta-
pa, fueron las de camé y cafetines. peluquerias. encomenderias. fondas. ete..
actividades todas ellas desempeñadas hasta entonces por industriales de
ctras nacionalidades como italianos. chinos. etc. y en las que el elemento
peruano ocupaba una proporcion verdaderamente insignificante.

Japanese activities in Peru:
REGARDING:
Information obtained from a Mr. Hector
de Paulet Wilquet, former Peruvian
Vice Consul at Yokohama, that a
change in Government of Peru may be
expected within 60 days inasmuch as
Fascist group in Peru has received
considerable financial aid from
Japanese Government.

$ir:
M WEILE
I have the honor to acknowledge the receipt of
the Department's strictly confidential instruction
No. 104 of March 30, 1938, inviting any comments I
may wish to make concerning a communication, under
date of March 11, 1938, from the Director of the
Federal Bureau of Investigation to the Secretary of
State.
The information which I have thus far been able
to gather creates grave doubt in my mind as to the
accuracy of the information furnished the Federal
Bureau
1

=== Page 283 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Bureau of Investigation by its informant. The statement
said to have been made by Mr. Wilquet that
"there is an existing contract between
Peruvian and Japanese interests, whereby
the Japanese ships call at those islands
(the Peruvian guano islands) for the pur-
pose of loading a certain quantity of
guano,"
is disproven by the fact that there is no such "existing
contract". The last contract for the sale of Peruvian
guano, as well s the last deliveries or shipments of
Peruvian guano to  apan or Japanese interests came to an
end in l932. I have been informed by a most reliable
source th-t during the p st six years no sales or ship-
ments of Peruvian guano have been made to Japan or Japanese
interests. Furthermore no guano has been sold or shipped
from Feru to any part of the world since  consignment in
1934 to american interests in the State of California,
with the exception of 5,ooo tons which were shipped to
England in l937; this consignment, however, was not re-
garded as real guano as th content was only 1% nitrogen-
ous, whereas real guano averages 12% nitrogen content.
Whatever rumors may have been current in Lima from
time to time during the past six montis concerning the
government, and making due allowance for the fact that
Lima is notorious for the wild rumors constantly in circu-
lation, there has not been the slightest evidence of such
a state of unrest or tension s to have justified the
prediction of a change of government " ithin the next
sixty days".
There is no evidence available that any group in
Peru has received "considerable financial aid directly or
indirectly

=== Page 284 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
indirectly from the Japanese Government."
While the Peruvian Government is deficient in coast-
guard vessels, the Japanese ships which make Peruvian
ports or ply by way of the Peruvian coast are fairly well
scrutinized in so far as concerns loading and unloading
operations. The Peruvian coast is virtually inaccessible
for landing purooses with the exception of the existing
harbors all of which are rigorously policed on shore.
While it would doubtless be possible to land small quanti-
ties of arms or munitions from time to time at points
not under direct surveillance, it would be extremely
difficult to put any considerable amount ashore without
detection, if for no other reason than that the e is no
continuous highway serving the shore and that the high-
ways which do exist are carefully policed, even small
bundles and packages carried under the arm or in automo-
biles being inspected by the highway police at frequent
intervals.
While it may be the fact that there is "only one
representative of the Peruvian Government on the island
where the guano is loaded" in the sense that there is
almost invariably one individual in charge of such an
operation, the fact remains that when guano is removed
a very large number of men are employed. It would be
extremely difficult to load any substantial quantity of
guano on Japanase ships without the f ct coming to the
attention of the Government as the present Government is
known to employ a very elaborate spy system. This latter
observation is made on the assumption that the loading
of

=== Page 285 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
of guano on Japanese ships at any time during the past six
years would have been unlawful. In this connection it is
worth noting that the removal and sale of guano is a
Government monopoly under the strictest control.
I understand that Hector de Paulet Wilquet is one of
the sons of Pedro de Paulet who is at present Chief of the
Commercial Division of the Foreign Office. I have learned
from a friend that Hector is regarded as the "black sheep"
of the family. One of the junior officials of the Foreign
Office of whom I inquired casually did not even know that
Mr. de Paulet of the Foreign Office had a son by the name
of Hector, indicating that the respectable members of the
family in Lim have studiously avoided disclosing the re-
lationship to their associates. Thus, the inform tion fur-
nished the Federal Bureau of Investigation concernin Hector
de Paulet Wilquet's irregularities appears to be supported by
the attitude of his family in Lima, and in the light of his
record, it would appear that little credence should be at-
tached to his statements.
As to the observation that one of the Consuls in the
Peruvian Consulate at Hongkong was in effect selling pass-
ports or visas for $1,500 each, I am advised that the Peru-
vian Foreign Office has for some time past suspected irreg-
ularities in that office, and that one of the motives for
the recent closing of the Consulate at Hongkong was the grave
suspicion that money was being demanded and accepted for the
issuance of passports or visas.
In

=== Page 286 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
In conclusion, it is my opinion that whatever
truth may ttacn to the statements made by Mr.
Wilquet concerning the irregular delivery of Peruvian
guano t Japanese interests in exchange for arms -
particularly rifles - must date back to a period prior
to 1932 when guano was being sold in substantial
quantities by Peru to Japan. It is entirely possible
that some such transactions may hav taken place at
that time, but if so it would appear that the practice
has been stopped for at least six years.
Respectfully yours,
auruce
LAS/ald
Laurence A. Steinhardt

I have the honor to inform the Department that Japan
continues to carry on its active propaganda campaign in
Peru.
The new Japanese inister is entertaining widely.
Yesterday, he gave a cocktail party for Engineer Manuel B.
Llosa and his Wife. r. Llosa is Teruvian Commissioner
to the Lew York and San Francisco World Fairs, as well as
Director of Fomento. ir. and Irs. Llosa departed today

no evenings open before he sailed.
The Minister then
asked him for luneheon, und received the same answer.
When he then suguested a cocktail vurty, : r. Llosa felt

While the latter may have

to cultivate these men, since this Ministry is the one

To me, the most significunt point is that the invitation

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

January 3, 1933.
EHD:
Santiago's despatoh No. 1337 of December
20, l932, reports that the Japanese Legation
in Chile has been disseminating pro-Japanese
